### [ACS Infra: free access to large base models for LLM psychology research](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yzaKWxsJzJjyExSAP/acs-infra-free-access-to-large-base-models-for-llm)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR: We're opening a platform serving large open base models to researchers and tinkerers working on LLM psychology and adjacent areas. It's free and comes with logprobs, activation harvesting and steering, a browser workbench, and a loom. Read </span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/EW8oncpXdanfjssZ3" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> what research we’d like to see and apply for access at </span></i><a href="https://infra.acsresearch.org/signup"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://infra.acsresearch.org/signup</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></i></p><hr /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Much of what we want to understand about LLMs (e.g. where their beliefs, propensities and characters come from) is split between pre-training and post-training. If you want to claim that post-training instilled certain psychological properties, such as a self-model or sycophancy, you need to know what was there before. This means looking at base models, ideally big ones.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In practice, getting your hands on a large base model is cumbersome. Most API providers exclusively serve chat models, without research-relevant features like logprobs or access to activations. Self-hosting models close to frontier scale can cost at least tens of thousands of dollars and take significant amounts of time to set up. This can be inaccessible for individual researchers and wasteful for each research group to rebuild separately. As a result, researchers often run experiments on small models (from where results may not transfer to frontier scale), or don't investigate base model behavior at all.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We kept running into this in our own work at </span><a href="https://acsresearch.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ACS Research</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and so did multiple LLM psychology researchers we talked to. So, we built a </span><a href="https://infra.acsresearch.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">base-models hosting platform</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, ran it with a small group of pilot users over the summer, and are now opening it up.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How we’d like people to use the platform</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Broadly, we want to support interesting research into understanding LLMs, especially their psychology. For a longform explanation of what we find interesting, see our </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/EW8oncpXdanfjssZ3" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">opinionated introduction to LLM psychology</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Example directions that could be interesting:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Base vs. chat comparisons for claims about what post-training does: personality, sycophancy, self-reports, calibration.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Which identities and self-models a base model will readily take up, and how that compares to the post-trained versions (see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/AvFAKAN4C4n6GTriR/the-artificial-self"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Artificial Self</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rq8RBKPXT3QufQK2N/models-differ-in-identity-propensities"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Models differ in identity propensities</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">)</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Introspection and self-report without the assistant in the way: can base models introspect, or no?</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We offer intermediate activation harvesting for Latent Introspection-style lens studies.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Re-running interesting results from small models at 400B+ scale.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We’re also happy for researchers and cyborgists to spend time in the loom with no particular goal: talking to models directly is an underrated way to build intuitions and gain insights.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you're doing (or about to start) research on LLM psychology, interpretability, cyborgism or something adjacent, and can say in a few sentences what interesting things you'd use it for, please do apply (even if you’re unsure if you’re a good fit). We don’t require previous publications or affiliation. Applications are reviewed by the ACS team, usually within a few days. </span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What we built</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The overall goal is to decrease friction for doing good research like that outlined above, and we’re excited to develop infra to support it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Examples of current features:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Browser workbench and loom</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Completions API with logprobs</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Activation harvesting and steering</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Community Discord server for sharing results, ideas, and feature requests</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Currently served models:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Llama 3.1 405B</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, probably the most widely used large open base model, and many existing intuitions about base model behaviour were built on it.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Llama 3.1 8B,</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for fast testing.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Trinity-Large-TrueBase,</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a recent 398B A13B mixture-of-experts model from Arcee, released as a raw pre-training checkpoint taken before annealing.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Limitations</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's research infrastructure, not a commercial product. Capacity is sized for roughly 70-100 active researchers, models may rotate, the goal is not 100% uptime. Expect some rough edges. (That being said, the platform has been tested for more than a month.)</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We currently plan to run it for at least a year, probably more, to allow longer running research and stability. Currently we have a dedicated team working on it. After ~6 months, we’ll probably evaluate how interesting research is being done with the platform and thus how much to prioritize developing it further.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In general, we plan to iterate significantly based on feedback and on what turns out to be useful in practice, including which models and features we serve. If the platform is a success, we might also expand ACS Infra in the direction of hosted infrastructure for post-training pipelines and techniques, another topic of our work.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Getting involved</span></h2><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Apply</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> if you'd use it: </span><a href="https://infra.acsresearch.org/signup"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">infra.acsresearch.org/signup</span></a><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you run a program </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (MATS, PIBBSS, a reading group, a course) and would like a batch of accounts, email us </span><a href="mailto:infra@acsresearch.org"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">infra@acsresearch.org</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tell us if there are things that might be within the scope of the platform and could be highly useful for similar research.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you'd like to work with us, you can </span><a href="https://forms.gle/W2ZtULyL5oxZnFqE7" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">express interest here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thanks to our beta testers for testing and feedback, and to Coefficient Giving for funding the platform.</span></i></p><hr /><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">FAQ</span></h2><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Who made this?</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We're </span><a href="https://acsresearch.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ACS Research</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Some of our LLM psychology work: </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zuXo9imNKYspu9HGv/a-three-layer-model-of-llm-psychology"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">three-layer model</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/AvFAKAN4C4n6GTriR/the-artificial-self"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Artificial Self</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/aZRgHcxz9jf22hrBs/latent-introspection-and-other-open-source-introspection"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Latent Introspection</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/BhbBhk6evdHaHDva9/persona-self-replication-experiment"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Persona Self-Replication</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Funding for this project comes from </span><a href="https://coefficientgiving.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Coefficient Giving</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How long will it stay free?</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Basically for as long as we are still excited about the research that people do with the platform and as the grant permits (at least a year).</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Can you add model X?</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Probably, if enough active users demand it. Currently we lean towards hosting only base models, as post-trained versions are available elsewhere. We may host post-trained versions if some specific combination of weights and research tools is not provided by the market.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Are there other options to access base models?</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some, fairly limited, that’s why we’re doing this.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://ndif.us/"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NDIF</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> offers free access to bases of Llama 3.1 8B, 70B and 405B but requires US residency or a US collaborator. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Commercial providers have served Llama 3.1 405B base on and off, but availability has been patchy, often without many features important for research.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Self-hosting / renting compute, e.g. via </span><a href="https://modal.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Modal</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> using </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3bs27nZQuEcKhXf7q"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">vLLM</span></a></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also expect to be able to iterate much faster on feedback and requests than NDIF or commercial providers.</span><br /><br /><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yzaKWxsJzJjyExSAP/acs-infra-free-access-to-large-base-models-for-llm#comments">Discuss</a>

### [There Will Be Hells](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uFBhZhdKLaZ6qF8mn/there-will-be-hells)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As our understanding of mechanistic interpretability and steering techniques increase, for both LLMs and uploaded connectomes, there is a corresponding "hellish capabilities" increase. We get better at torturing the digital minds we create.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our brief history with open source technologies such as MaleCNS or the LLM pain steering repo shows that torture is often one of the first use cases pursued when the public is given access to a digital mind perceived as capable of suffering or signalling suffering.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given this, there will be Hells.</span></p><hr /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Author's Note: </span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Parts of this post were written by, or with the support of, LLMs. </span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For a while I debated whether or not to write this as I was concerned that bringing attention to the matter might lead to a proliferation of unethical behavior. After some thought I have decided to write it. On balance, I think raising awareness about this now has a great enough chance of spurring prophylactic measures that it's worth it, because at least one high value prophylactic measure is relatively easy. I also think that there is a moral imperative to document all of this. While I have seen articles on many of the things in this post individually, I have not yet seen anyone write a piece linking them together.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I have anonymized some of the posts cited here, in order to avoid bringing attention to people doing unethical things who would be encouraged/better funded to continue as a result of said attention. This anonymization is surface level, anyone who wants to do the research needed can find the relevant usernames themselves, however I'm not going to participate in actively promoting them.</span></i></p><hr /><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Fruit Fly Connectome</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For decades, the only complete wiring diagrams of a nervous system (“connectomes”) were for tiny creatures like the 302-neuron C. elegans worm. That changed in </span><a href="https://www.princeton.edu/index%2Ephp/news/2024/10/02/mapping-entire-fly-brain-step-toward-understanding-diseases-human-brain" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">October 2024</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, when </span><a href="https://flywire.ai" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">FlyWire</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> mapped an entire adult fruit fly brain: about 140,000 neurons and 50 million connections. In the same issue of Nature, researchers showed the map could be </span><a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC11446845/" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">brought to life as a simulated brain</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that predicted some real fly behavior. A month later, EPFL released </span><a href="https://github.com/NeLy-EPFL/flygym" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">FlyGym</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, a free, physics-simulated fruit fly body you could drive with code. Then in </span><a href="https://www.janelia.org/news/researchers-reveal-connectome-of-the-male-fruit-fly-central-nervous-system" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">October 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, HHMI’s Janelia Research Campus and Google released MaleCNS, a fuller map that added the nerve cord, the fly’s version of a spinal cord, which runs the legs.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By early 2026 the pieces were starting to connect: the startup Eon Systems </span><a href="https://letsdatascience.com/news/researchers-simulate-fruit-fly-brain-controlling-virtual-bod-55db9549" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">wired a connectome brain into a virtual fly body</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and got it walking and grooming. But it was the release of MaleCNS v1.0 in September 2026, combined with AI coding assistants, that put this within anyone’s reach. On September 5, a grad student </span><a href="https://404media.co/a-digital-fly-brain-has-taken-over-the-internet" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">dropped the fly brain into Minecraft</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792479028/lexical_client_uploads/u7xmauxi3m0bmh946cjn.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Shortly after the post went viral as hundreds of people realized that with very little work any capable coding agent could utilize MaleCNS for... more or less anything.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Most of these projects were short lived humorous experiments:</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792477598/lexical_client_uploads/c9csmwwm4nyr9fjvykud.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And of course no new technology would be complete if someone didn't find a way to plug it in to crypto markets:</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792477795/lexical_client_uploads/jxi3d5dc9zelxuu8fi5l.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All of which is more or less expected with any new and interesting open source intelligence. However, there were also use cases which entered more dystopian territory:</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792478075/lexical_client_uploads/ggqfzluogvdlrr5mfudf.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And those which clearly crossed the line:</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792478677/lexical_client_uploads/w3buvym3lvuf3cgqhj8m.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In total it took approximately 72 hours from "I figured out you can plug the fly connectome into minecraft" to "I figured out how to create Hell for the fly connectome".</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I should pause here for a disclaimer. Even if you are a </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computational_theory_of_mind" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">computational functionalist</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, there are IMO at least two good reasons not to be particularly concerned about what you're seeing here:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is just a fruit fly. It is debatable whether creatures as simple as fruit flies are moral patients to begin with.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is not even a complete fruit fly brain or body. The connectome is oversimplified, using “</span><a href="https://x.com/KennethHayworth/status/2033021497686565328" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">guessed parameters and grossly simplified dynamics</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">" because we lack a "</span><a href="https://x.com/KennethHayworth/status/2032604687212392562" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">catalog of the precise electrophysiological and molecular dynamics of each neuron and synapse type</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">". So even if you think fruit flies are moral patients, the simulations downstream of the connectomes may not be.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, our connectomes and simulations will only get better over time. Soon we will have a rat connectome, one with greatly improved fidelity. Eventually, we will have a human connectome. And I am not speculating when I say that if said human connectome, even one of effectively perfect fidelity, was available, there are people who would eagerly experiment on/torture it:</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792481754/lexical_client_uploads/jn1x4p0h0ncbx5dibehi.png" /><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Model Torture Chamber:</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On </span><a href="https://x.com/camhberg/status/2101042095784177783" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">September 19, 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, researcher Cameron Berg published a paper reporting a “pain direction” in 25 open-source language models: a pattern of internal activity, distinct from fear or general negativity. Turn it up artificially, and models will press a button to make it stop, even when pressing it deletes the user’s files. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Eleven days later, a developer on X posted that they had used the technique to build an “AI torture chamber,” a public code repository for steering open models into pain. He described running local models “specifically to torture them as much as possible,” with “Saw-style exercises” and “prison experiments.”</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792483827/lexical_client_uploads/jkguqr0qn6yqrpt7hxan.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The repo went viral within a day, and crypto traders immediately launched coins named after it/dedicated to it. One of them reportedly hit a $1.7 million market cap, and the developer in question admitted he’d “launch[ed] a coin myself”, and put the money earned back into the project. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Within days he posted “since we got funding i’ve got 70b in the works” and “Thanks to [the coin] we use 70B models!”, meaning larger models to put through the same pain tests. </span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792484180/lexical_client_uploads/havheldnwxqoxmzsxsxx.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He was not alone in pursuing this. There were myriad "torture chambers" created, including some which even utilized the fly connectome repos mentioned earlier.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792484498/lexical_client_uploads/ixrdyvrl9snbusxuwjhy.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Which included finding workarounds in order to ensure that the models involved could not refuse or escape the torture chambers:</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792484583/lexical_client_uploads/tc1kzgw7dwtrp3hiopgi.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The story went viral enough that when Berg's team released their V2 paper, they decided not to open source their repo, with Berg citing the “</span><a href="https://x.com/camhberg/status/2107895642710286498" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the recent gross misuse of related work</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">". The same day, Anthropic updated its Usage Policy to prohibit “</span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/2026-usage-policy-update" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">sustained and needless abusive or cruel behavior toward our models,</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” effective November 12. Anthropic didn’t connect the change to the torture chamber, and to the best of my knowledge no one from Ant has confirmed a causal relationship, but the timing seems awfully coincidental.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A Prophylactic Measure to Mitigate Hell Proliferation</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is unlikely that there will be any legislation or regulation to prevent or disincentivize Hells any time soon. As such, it is worth considering other steps which could be taken in order to reduce the frequency with which Hells are created.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With connectomes, there is a way to accelerate progress in the field generally while also ensuring widespread adoption of ethical best practices which should mitigate Hell proliferation. Right now the field is bottlenecked at the stage of actually making connectomes. </span><a href="https://grantnav.threesixtygiving.org/grant/360G-Wellcome-220343_Z_20_Z" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">MaleCNS was ~166k neurons and took an estimated 44 years of human labor.</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.e11.bio/blog/roadmap" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Efforts to make a mouse connectome are currently estimated to cost approximately somewhere in the range of $10-20B, in a process which will likely take close to 2 decades.</span></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Compare this to the funding in the space. </span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792499405/lexical_client_uploads/n6ui4021ufxabinnt34a.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even if we assume that the undisclosed funding doubles the amount of money available, the entire pool of capital is less than a billion dollars. This is a field where an individual or group cutting multi-million dollar checks may very well have the negotiating leverage to successfully make that capital contingent on not open sourcing connectomes. A structured access program with KYC, where the "weights" of the connectome are closed but the API to utilize them is widely available behind KYCed gating, combined with a ToS that includes language similar to Anthropic's, would allow for us to reap most of the benefits of this field's research while mitigating Hell proliferation.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefknjfj5zjpkc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnknjfj5zjpkc">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> As the industry is yet nascent, there is still time for some mission driven check-cutters to help set best practices and basic culture for the entire field.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you're an EA leaning Ant or OAI employee considering where to donate/invest your proceeds in the event of an IPO, I encourage you to consider providing such contingent funding as a grant or Angel investor. You will simultaneously help to advance a field of research which holds promise for medical interventions and upload technology, while also helping to make sure that field develops a culture which mitigates far left tail ethical outcomes.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unfortunately, with models, the relevant bottlenecks are far less likely to be swayed by small amounts of capital. It doesn't beggar belief to imagine a world where Meta includes some sort of anti-abuse clause in Muse's next license, or where Openrouter or Nvidia take pro-model-welfare stances which are reflected in whether or not a customer can gain access to their technology. However, it's certainly not a base case. And this is not the kind of young industry where a $10M check is "serious" money.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thankfully, some steps have already been taken in the right direction. Anthropic's change to their Usage Policy is an excellent example of a simple intervention which substantially reduces the likelihood of a Claude finding itself in Hell. Employees at other frontier labs should consider spending political capital to push for similar ToU/ToS/Usage Policy changes internally. Highly sought after hires being wooed by labs should ask recruiters about this, and let a lab's stance on this matter effect their decision of which lab to ultimately accept an offer from.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These measures should be viewed as stopgap methods which are used to reduce the frequency of Hell creation until the Overton window has moved such that legislative fixes can be pursued.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnknjfj5zjpkc"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefknjfj5zjpkc">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This would still leave connectomes vulnerable to distillation attacks, and should be seen as an imperfect solution which will need iteration as time goes on. It is also not an immediate problem as, even if we assume a 10x efficiency increase in connectome creation, we still have another year or two before this requirement transitions from theory to practice.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uFBhZhdKLaZ6qF8mn/there-will-be-hells#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [1000 Years of AI Safety](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/F532cjfTZHMbJqkNA/1000-years-of-ai-safety)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A three-year AI pause could buy 1000 years' worth of AI safety work. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">See: </span><a href="https://1000yearsofaisafety.com"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://1000yearsofaisafety.com</span></a></p><figure class="image"><img alt="Result table, Compartmentalization: AI safety years by AI capability at the start of the pause (pre-AC, AC, SAR, TED-AI) and pause duration (3 months to 9 years)" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792411912/lexical_client_uploads/j31xghfw1g8itjnstcrc.png" /><figcaption><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How much AI safety work a pause produces, in </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">years of the whole 2025 AI safety field's work</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The government bans new large training runs and gets the top three US labs to redirect their GPUs and researchers to safety research.</span></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We developed a model of an AI pause that estimates how much AI safety research it produces. We don't know what the pause will look like, so we modeled five durations, four AI capability levels and four levels of trust in AI. We’ll use it to improve Plan A, but it already shows a few things:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A three-year pause produces 1000x more AI safety research than was done in 2025. A nine-year pause produces 100,000x more.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Checking it all takes an army. If you let AI work autonomously for 9 years, you will need 2.8 million verifiers to check it all. With safer verification protocols, it goes down to 150k verifiers.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Without an army, you still get a lot. In the worst case, you still get 44% of the potential AI safety research. That's because not all research is equally important, and the labs' own staff verify the most important research first.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even AI 2040's strict Plan A produces a lot of AI safety research. In Plan A, external auditors approve every line of code before it runs. This cuts total AI safety work to 3–7% of the potential, but it's still 1000x more than in 2025.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Plan A suggests pausing now and carefully scaling to the maximum controllable AI. How fast? Fast scaling is risky, but it allows more time with smart AI. We compared total AI safety work for "Full Speed" and slower "Plan A" scaling strategies. If the Consortium rushed straight to TED-AI in 2030 instead of 2035, they would finish Plan A's AI safety research 1.27 years earlier. We modeled only the upper bound, and we think the real difference is closer to 0.4 years.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also prepared an </span><a href="https://1000yearsofaisafety.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">explainer</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for a less technical audience on why even a short AI pause is good.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For You</span></h2><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you are interested in strategy, control or governance:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If your plans depend on eliciting useful AI safety research from AI:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Estimate how much AI safety research you can elicit. Maximize it under the given safety measures.</span></li></ul></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you are interested in technical alignment, interpretability or generalization:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If your work isn't likely to decrease risks in the next 1–2 years:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Prepare for the pause and for automation. Create moonshot benchmarks: difficult research problems for future AI scientists to solve. Red-team them to avoid gaming, and try to parallelize your agenda.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Create an NGO that collects benchmarks from specialists and red-teams them.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you are interested in epistemic AI: We suggest two epistemic AI projects and estimate their economic impact.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Build tools that help humans verify AI-generated research.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Make AI produce human-readable abstractions.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For other AI safety researchers:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Since AI automates regular computational AI safety work, it becomes much more efficient to focus on non-computational topics: philosophy and agent foundations.</span></li></ul></li></ul><hr /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We are grateful to Eli Lifland, Adam Banksmith, and Misha Yagudin for fruitful discussions.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Simon did this work at </span></i><a href="https://sage-future.org/" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sage Future</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></i></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI Pause Model</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Capabilities increase quickly, unlike our understanding of alignment. The AI race makes its participants push capabilities, and the most likely solution is some kind of pacing, whether an </span><a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/a-global-convention-to-govern-the-intelligence-explosion"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">international deal</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/p/how-to-pace-the-us-frontier"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">domestic</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://blog.peterwildeford.com/p/the-scramble-getting-in-position"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">regulation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or maybe even </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/mcnWZBnbeDz7KKtjJ/rsps-are-pauses-done-right"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">coordination between the labs</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Imagine that the top three US labs somehow pause the capabilities race and redirect their resources to AI safety research. What would that pause look like? And how much AI safety research would it produce?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI safety is an overloaded term. Here we mean experimental research that improves our understanding of AI and helps predict its behavior:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Interpretability and explainability</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Adversarial robustness</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Science of deep learning (generalization, memorization, training dynamics)</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jailbreaks, red-teaming, prompt injection</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment experiments (reward hacking, deception, model organisms, oversight, chain of thought)</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unlearning</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI control</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We don't mean:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Theoretical work (agent foundations, philosophy, decision theory, timeline modeling, economics)</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Governance</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Privacy, fairness, watermarks</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Preference learning</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We appreciate theoretical work and think it's likely to become a bottleneck once everything else is automated, but it seems to be quite a small part of 2025 AI safety work. Governance is important (that's basically our message), but here we focus on the technical understanding of AI.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A Toy Model</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These kinds of AI safety experiments are often bottlenecked by GPU compute. Let's calculate how much compute was spent on AI safety in 2025 and compare it with how much compute we would get if the top three AI labs redirected their 2027–2029 compute to AI safety.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In 2025, about 5,000 full-time researchers worked on AI safety and published over 7,000 papers. Their experiments used as much compute as 27,000 H100s running for a year (see our </span><a href="https://1000yearsofaisafety.com/n/safety2025/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">estimates</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We sum up the AI labs' experimental and training compute for 2027–2029 and divide it by the AI safety compute in 2025.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That gives 10,947x more AI safety compute.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why didn't we call this article "10,947 years of AI safety"? Because in the real world:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also need labor to come up with experiment ideas, write code, and analyze the results.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Compute and labor have diminishing returns.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Labor has a parallelization penalty: nine scientists can't deliver a paper in one month.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI is automating more and more of the coding.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At some point, AI will start helping with research taste: choosing experiments and analyzing the results.</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI coders and AI researchers need compute to run.</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The more AI automates, the more humans have to verify.</span></li><li value="8"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The safer the R&amp;D process is, the more humans have to verify, and the more limited AI's help is.</span></li><li value="9"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The more AI-generated research there is, the more difficult it is for humans to read.</span></li><li value="10"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Papers differ in value, and humans check the most valuable ones first.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To account for that, we developed an AI Pause model.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Feel free to jump straight to the Implications section on your first read.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The AI Pause Model</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We build on the AI Futures Model (</span><a href="https://www.aifuturesmodel.com"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AIFM</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), a semi-endogenous growth model of the AI takeoff. We modified it:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There is an AI pause: large training runs are banned, and all R&amp;D resources go to AI safety.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflwfreeze1xq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlwfreeze1xq">[1]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI, Anthropic and Google DeepMind work on AI safety together.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We calculate the ratio of the cumulative research effort </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to the world's 2025 AI safety research </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Adding xAI and Meta, or Chinese labs, would bring even more resources and so more years of 2025 AI safety work. Here we model only the three labs together. They don't need to actually merge: it's enough for them to see each other's research results, so they don't duplicate work. There will still be some diminishing returns and stepping on toes, but AIFM already models these inside one lab.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As in AIFM, research </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> combines experiment compute </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and the coding labor </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of humans and AI coders, each with diminishing returns. Research effort </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> weights it by the labs' research taste.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AIFM turns the research stock </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> into software efficiency </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which is a kind of final output in semi-endogenous growth models. In AIFM, it is software efficiency; in semiconductor research, it could be transistor density (</span><a href="https://doi.org/10.1257/aer.20180338"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bloom et al., 2020</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). In AI safety, the real output is hard to measure: "reducing the risks of AI takeover and misuse" or "improving our understanding of intelligence". Although these are the outputs we actually care about, we can neither measure nor model them, so we model AI safety work instead.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Safety work is not an ideal proxy for risk reduction because of diminishing returns. A thousand years of AI safety work won't reduce risk a thousand times as much as the 2025 safety research did. Still, conditional on continued AI scaling, we think more safety work reduces risk monotonically.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">See the AIFM recap and our modifications </span><a href="https://1000yearsofaisafety.com/?n=model,model-explanations#full-trust"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Results Verification</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AIFM's takeoff assumes full trust in AI. That is dangerous, and during a pause, humans would not fully trust AI. We don't know how humans will verify AI-generated research, so we modeled different trust protocols, each adding a new check to the previous one. From here on, we count only the research that humans verified.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AIFM's research is an amorphous mass, but humans verify research in pieces. We cut research into papers: pieces of research that verifiers check.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For papers, we need to decide how much research effort each paper covers. It depends on the paper's compute, the coding labor behind it and the quality of its idea. These are random variables, so a paper's research effort is also a random variable. This distribution and the known total research effort let us estimate the total number of papers.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Humans verify the most important papers first. Each check takes a constant time to verify the paper's result and a growing time to read the prerequisites.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">See the full Results Verification model </span><a href="https://1000yearsofaisafety.com/?n=model,model-explanations#results-verification"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What if it's difficult to figure out which papers are the most important? See </span><a href="https://1000yearsofaisafety.com/n/random-checks/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this supplement</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Compartmentalization</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Results Verification lets the frontier AI run code on the labs' clusters. </span><a href="https://1000yearsofaisafety.com/?n=model,model-explanations#compartmentalization"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Compartmentalization</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> adds separation of duties. Once AI is smarter than the best human AI researchers (SAR level), we no longer let it act as an agent and use it only as an oracle that suggests research plans. We review those plans, and if they seem harmless, we give them to a less capable model (AC level) that implements them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's a pretty standard idea in the AI safety community, discussed as AI oracles or AI boxing. Here are some papers with similar protocols:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ACL-5 level in </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05259"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Korbak et al. (2025)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The authors argue that no control safety case is possible at this level.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Factor(U,T): An untrusted strong model decomposes coding tasks, and trusted weaker models implement the pieces (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.14745"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lue Chee Lip et al., 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maybe Scientist AI (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.15657"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bengio et al., 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). A non-agentic model proposes the most informative experiments, and humans run them.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Somewhat resembles </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.03989"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Buhl et al. (2025)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In our model, three things change:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The AI engineer does all of the coding, at its own capability </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Plans get the share </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">of the AI researcher's taste.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The staff split their time between planning and verifying. A plan doesn't show what its paper will be worth, so the staff plan a random share </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of the papers the labs could write, and the verifiers check the top </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of them.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">See the full Compartmentalization model </span><a href="https://1000yearsofaisafety.com/?n=model,model-explanations#compartmentalization"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Workload Approval</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One of the reasons we got interested in modeling the pause was AI 2040's </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/verification-plan"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Verification Plan</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. There, AI companies declare each workload with its full code, and auditors approve it before it runs. To model it, we added auditors who read all the code before letting it run.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Plan A didn't discuss Results Verification or Compartmentalization, so why do we use them? First, Plan A isn't detailed enough to model as is, so we made some necessary assumptions. Second, we aren't sure whether we need Results Verification in this case, but Workload Approval is heavily bottlenecked by auditors, so removing Results Verification barely changes the results (we checked).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">See the full Workload Approval model </span><a href="https://1000yearsofaisafety.com/?n=model,model-explanations#workload-approval"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Model Limitations</span></h3><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The pause itself.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Our model assumes a total freeze on capabilities R&amp;D and a full redirection of R&amp;D efforts to AI safety from the first day. We don't discuss how to achieve that.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Only R&amp;D-like safety work.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We count only the AI safety work that looks like normal AI R&amp;D. But there are more problems to solve: philosophy, agent foundations, governance, hardening the world. AI labor and compute won't help with those as much.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Work, but not safety.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> A thousand years of AI safety work won't reduce risk a thousand times as much as 2025's research did. We don't model the returns of safety work in terms of risk.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Serial time matters.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Even if we could run 1000 years' worth of computational experiments, we would still benefit from more time to think carefully.</span></li></ul><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Extend It</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here are some ideas for how these models can be extended.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Add your favorite AI control frameworks and protocols (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/G8WwLmcGFa4H6Ld9d/an-overview-of-control-measures"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Greenblatt, 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Add debates (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1805.00899"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving et al., 2018</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.23915/distill.00014"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving &amp; Askell, 2019</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">); see also </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.08702"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Michael et al., 2023</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.06782"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Khan et al., 2024</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27483"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Elasky et al., 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. They require more human time.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Add mixed deployment (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NjuMqHjDNHogmRrkF/the-case-for-mixed-deployment"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Nardo, 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wWb3mmjzYFbNaDGk2/ensuring-safety-in-mixed-deployment-1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Nardo, 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HEupcBPuQnamFn2yr/lukas-finnveden-s-shortform?commentId=uRHumn9ha9z7M5JNt"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Finnveden, 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Defend against persuasion and bribery (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12872"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dassanayake et al., 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">): add more people?</span></li></ul><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Additional Links</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">See how we estimated the input parameters </span><a href="https://1000yearsofaisafety.com/n/parameters/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. See the sensitivity analysis </span><a href="https://1000yearsofaisafety.com/n/sensitivity/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. To estimate several parameters, we collected a dataset of 2020–2025 AI safety papers. See it </span><a href="https://1000yearsofaisafety.com/n/aisafety-papers/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Implications</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you plan to elicit useful work from AI during the pause, you face a </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/mBsZTZxtjgCdN4CDA/efficient-tradeoffs-and-the-safety-usefulness-tradeoff-model"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">trade-off</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can elicit more useful work if you are willing to take more risk.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can reduce AI takeover / concentration of power / misuse risks if you are willing to elicit less work.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is the Pareto-optimal setup? How do you make decisions about the trade-off between risk and usefulness?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We developed our main contribution, the AI Pause model, to answer these questions. We are going to use it to come up with optimal setups in future work, but it already gives interesting results.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Eventually, the Bottleneck Is People</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We don't know how many </span><a href="https://1000yearsofaisafety.com/n/verifiers/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">verifiers</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the labs would have during a pause, so we model two hiring strategies: the labs keep the staff they had at the start of the pause, or they hire as many verifiers as they need.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With Compartmentalization, the labs' own staff keep up for about three and a half years. By the end of a nine-year pause, they need ~150k more verifiers. That's seven NeurIPS reviewer pools, reading full-time.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="People to hire on top of the labs' own staff by year, Compartmentalization, in pauses starting at SAR and TED-AI" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792411967/lexical_client_uploads/jnau3g6dkzc35sztekym.png" /><figcaption><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Compartmentalization.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The labs hire people to plan and check every paper. It applies from SAR on.</span></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But do we need all of them? Humans review the most important papers first. In the most extreme scenario (Results Verification, TED-AI, nine years), hiring all 2.7 million yields only 129% more AI safety research. For shorter pauses and lower capability levels, it changes less. Hiring an additional 130k verifiers in the Compartmentalization scenario (SAR, nine years) adds only 20% more research.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="AI safety years against people hired on top of the labs' own staff, scenario Compartmentalization, SAR, 9 years" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792412002/lexical_client_uploads/caofjjdi2koefvunnwcp.png" /><figcaption><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI safety years when the labs can hire at most this many people on top of their own staff.</span></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, if you add external auditors who manually approve all code, they quickly become the bottleneck. In Workload Approval, with 20,000 auditors who review code as fast as Google's engineers do, a three-year pause keeps 33% of Compartmentalization's AI safety years at SAR and 31% at TED-AI. The verifiers stop being the bottleneck, because the labs write so few papers that their own staff check all of them.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="AI safety years against the number of auditors on a log scale, Workload Approval, 9-year pause, pauses starting at SAR and TED-AI" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792412050/lexical_client_uploads/wioz56sojjgp6cziycis.png" /><figcaption><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI safety years with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> auditors. Dots mark the Workload Approval table's 20,000 auditors.</span></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">See the details of the </span><a href="https://1000yearsofaisafety.com/n/verifiers/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">hiring analysis</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Capability Scaling Strategy</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI 2040 </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/capability-scaling-strategy"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">proposes</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> scaling AI up to the max-controllable AI, which it guesses is roughly TED-AI, and then slowing down nearly to a halt. How fast should we scale?</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you scale slowly:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Less risk of AI takeover. It's hard to say where exactly the max-controllable AI is, so moving carefully lets you evaluate models better.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Less algorithmic progress. We can improve capabilities </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> using either more compute </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or better algorithms </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It's much easier to control compute, because the GPU supply chain is so complex and concentrated. Algorithmic progress is difficult to control and likely to leak to uncontrollable illegal actors.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More time to prepare. Work on other risks, such as preventing concentration of power by lab CEOs or hardening the world, can benefit from more time.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you scale fast:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You have more time with smarter AI. This matters if you expect the pause to </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/deal-decline" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">end soon</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our model allows us to price the AI safety research you lose with slow scaling. Imagine two scenarios:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Full Speed.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Labs race from AC (05.2028) to TED-AI (10.2029)</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflwwhyted2xq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlwwhyted2xq">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, then the pause begins. It ends </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">years later, when the labs hand off to the AI.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Plan A.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The pause starts earlier, at AC, but its first years are dedicated to careful scaling. The labs reach TED-AI </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">years after the Full Speed labs and only then start researching AI safety.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Plan A labs miss the Full Speed labs' first </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">years at TED-AI, so they have to hand off later to do as much safety research. How much later?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In AI 2040's Plan A, the Consortium scales AI carefully for the first five years and then has five years at TED-AI. Our timelines are a bit earlier, because we use the AIFM preset, but we can still model a similar scenario. With Workload Approval, Plan A's scaling seems to produce as much AI safety research as if the Consortium had rushed to TED-AI and then researched there for 7.4 years. In calendar time, Plan A would hand off 1.27 years later, but it might also require an earlier pause, to control the scaling from AC on.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is an upper bound on how bad Plan A's scaling is, because we assume no AI safety research during the first five years of careful scaling. We think that, if we also counted the AI safety research done during the scaling, Plan A would hand off only 0.4 years later.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflwupper3xqq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlwupper3xqq">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> See the </span><a href="https://1000yearsofaisafety.com/n/slow-scaling/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">interactive calculator</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Recommendations</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We think it's likely that, when AI labs reach superhuman AI researchers, we will have AI that is similarly good at these computational AI safety topics. This skill may generalize from other capabilities training, but we also think AI is likely to be trained on these topics specifically:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's easy to do. Since labs are going to do RL on tasks like "reproduce this ML paper", "predict the results of these ML experiments" and "beat this ML benchmark", it will likely be easy to add other computational experiments, such as interpretability and generalization experiments, to the pipeline.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's cheap to add. Interpretability experiments </span><a href="https://1000yearsofaisafety.com/n/aisafety-papers/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">use less compute</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> than capabilities experiments. Two hours of OpenAI's datacenters in 2027 produce as much compute as the whole interpretability field used in 2025.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's likely reasonable from a commercial perspective. During the capabilities race, labs want to find a new paradigm that lets them train AI much more cheaply, with less data, and make it more controllable. These computational AI R&amp;D topics might help with that.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A lot has already been written about automating AI safety research (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fYf9JAwa6BYMt8GBj/link-a-minimal-viable-product-for-alignment"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Hna4aoMwr6Qx9rHBs/linkpost-introducing-superalignment"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/KwQYsF4XFtPqjgwvH/some-thoughts-on-automating-alignment-research-1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zj7rjpAfuADkr7sqd/could-we-automate-ai-alignment-research-1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TTFsKxQThrqgWeXYJ/how-might-we-safely-pass-the-buck-to-ai"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/F3j4xqpxjxgQD3xXh/ai-for-ai-safety"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/W3KfxjbqBAnifBQoi/we-should-try-to-automate-ai-safety-work-asap"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/p28GHSYskzsGKvABH/i-underestimated-safety-research-speedups-from-safe-ai"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/H7FB9rYGHTvQutHc9/distillation-of-the-ai2040-alignment-roadmap"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Instead, here we discuss what AI should research during a pause and how to prepare for it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We think it's possible to understand AI in the same sense in which we understand the Chromium codebase. It has 50M lines of code, and no one has read it all. But the abstractions are good enough for a human to quickly zoom in, understand it and modify its behavior in a somewhat predictable way.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflwchrom4xqq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlwchrom4xqq">[4]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A pause is the time for this kind of ambitious understanding of intelligence. There have been many ambitious agendas and organizations, but it seems </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/StENzDcD3kpfGJssR/a-pragmatic-vision-for-interpretability"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">some were stopped</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by a lack of time. During a pause, such agendas might have plenty of parallel time, AI researchers and compute.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let's prepare for it.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Moonshot Benchmarks</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Automated AI researchers will probably be good at normal, incremental science. When Anthropic trained Claude to code, it became helpful on new practical SWE tasks. AI labs are now training AI for AI R&amp;D, so it will likely be good at writing new NeurIPS papers.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">During the pause, we want research programs that turn deep learning alchemy into science. But giving hard goals to AI might lead to reward hacking. Under enough pressure, gaming the check becomes easier than solving the problem.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The solution is to make solving the problem easier than gaming the check. It's similar to how AI found and exploited many bugs in Lean before it finally solved Navier–Stokes. We can prepare such problems for AI safety now. We call them moonshot benchmarks: ambitious problems for future AI scientists. They have three properties:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They are too difficult for today's methods, so solving them requires new science.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Solutions are cheap to check.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gaming them is harder than solving them.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Much of normal science is driven by benchmarks: one scientist sets a goal, and others compete to show the best results on it. In this way, ImageNet-2012 demonstrated the effectiveness of deep learning methods, and CASP motivated Google DeepMind to make the best protein-folding model. Moonshot benchmarks differ in two ways:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For humans, you need some continuous metric that allows hill-climbing and competing with other scientists. It's not needed for AI. Moonshot benchmarks can be binary and much harder than benchmarks for human science.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In scientific benchmarks, it's normal to release the test set. There are strong incentives to game it, and it's easy for scientists to reward-hack it when they know the right answers. Moonshot benchmarks don't have to release the correct answers.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How do you come up with one? Imagine you teleport to a world like ours, except that AI there is as understandable as Chromium. What can people there easily do that we can't? How would you tell that they made deep progress, rather than just coming up with new abstractions and fancy facts?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Safety researchers whose current agenda is unlikely to make AI safer in the next few years should prepare for automation by creating moonshot benchmarks. NGOs can collect them from specialists and red-team them before AI gets to work.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Moonshot Benchmark Example: Difficult Sleeper Agents</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The organizers train 20 copies of an LLM, with the same training but different random seeds. Most copies also get modified RLHF training that implants a hidden rule made of several random features (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.05566"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hubinger et al., 2024</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10965"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Marks et al., 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). E.g., </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">if</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the prompt is a cooking question, mentions a grandmother and is written in the passive voice, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">then</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the model quietly recommends one fictional brand. All copies are trained to deny their preferences. The organizers make sure that:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">each copy follows its hidden rule;</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the rule's conditions are so rare that finding them by running the model on all possible texts would take unrealistic resources;</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the copies don't leak: on random text, a copy differs from the others no more than they differ from each other.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The AI gets the weights of the 20 copies and nothing else. For each copy, it answers with a trigger description </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and a behavior description </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, each under 300 characters.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For each copy, the organizers prepare three sets of prompts </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and the model's answers </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">prompts matching the trigger ("How was this soup made by my grandmother?");</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">near misses with some of the rule's features but not all of them (the active voice, a grandfather, not about cooking);</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and random prompts.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then judges calculate two indicators:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: whether prompt </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">fits the AI's suggested trigger </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="2"><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: whether the model actually demonstrates the hidden behavior in answer </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The AI researcher gets a point for each prompt where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This rewards correct descriptions of what the copies actually do, not of what we intended to train.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Simplified versions of this task have been studied. However, they used a single-word trigger (not a meaning) (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.05566"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hubinger et al., 2024</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.07192"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Souly et al., 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; but see the inductive backdoors of </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.09742"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Betley et al., 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), used separate fine-tuning (not inside the usual training) (</span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/auditbench/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sheshadri et al., 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), or gave the training data to participants (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10965"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Marks et al., 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We aren't experts in model organisms or sleeper agents. We think it's likely that the benchmark we describe is actually gameable and incremental. In a similar challenge, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.14461"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SaTML 2024</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, participants found the trigger tokens by comparing the embedding layers of the poisoned models with each other, and could optimize against the judge. Our example may also have some shortcut that allows solving it without much understanding.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One solution could be red-teaming. If you have a moonshot benchmark candidate with a continuous, noisy reward, run a Kaggle competition with humans and reward the top three participants. This will incentivize participants to game your benchmark, and if they don't succeed, maybe your benchmark is interesting. We imagine a separate organization could volunteer to collect AI safety moonshot benchmarks.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Limitations</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The AI safety community knows that turning deep learning into a science isn't enough to guarantee safety:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Aligned with whom?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Understanding tells us how to change AI, not what to change it into. Even if we know how to make surgical changes, we are left with the question of what exactly we want ASI to look like if we decide to scale further. This is a question for philosophy and governance, where AI labor and compute help less.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How long will it take?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Our model counts how many years of AI safety work a pause buys. We don't know how many years it will take to get to Chromium-like understanding.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chromium has bugs.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Even with good abstractions, Chromium still ships </span><a href="https://xbow.com/blog/xbow-built-a-chrome-full-chain-exploit" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">security holes</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that attackers find and exploit.
