With very smart AI, we won't get a chance to patch it after release, so it should get into the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fkLYhTQteAu5SinAc/corrigibility"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">alignment basin</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on the first try.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Still, it's better to have this kind of understanding than not to have it at all.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Epistemic AI Projects</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We give two consequentialist recommendations on increasing the efficiency of human verifiers to get the most out of AI safety research during the pause.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Verifier Uplift</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Develop tools and methodologies to make adversarial review shorter for human verifiers. In our model, a review that's </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">times faster is worth as much as </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">times more verifiers.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ML conferences are already a great testbed for adversarial review. Scientists try to make their work look more impressive than it is and hope that reviewers won't read the code.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Formats.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> There has been a lot of research on alternative scientific media</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgm6e3f848nv"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngm6e3f848nv">[5]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to make sharing and reading research easier. But it seems that academic incentives don't always reward clarity. Clarity might make papers look less impressive to peer reviewers.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If, during an AI pause, research is done by AI, we might care less about impressiveness and status attribution, and more about findings. If we reward verifiers for finding errors in AI research, it incentivizes clarity.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure out what media formats make it easier for human researchers to find errors and bugs in ML research. Run experiments with real researchers. Maybe collect human feedback data and use it to train AI that is clearer in scientific communication.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tools.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Build scaffolds over weaker trusted models to check and dumb down ML research: both papers and code. Build a tool to help human reviewers find bugs and wrong statements faster. Use this tool to scan existing famous papers and their repositories. Can you find new errors in famous papers? If you can demonstrate it, you are likely to gain the trust of conference reviewers.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Better Abstractions</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As AI does research autonomously, it will come up with new abstractions. They won't necessarily be elegant to human minds. In fact, they aren't in 2026. Tristan Buckmaster, one of the disputed coauthors of the AI-assisted Navier–Stokes work, </span><a href="https://cims.nyu.edu/~tristanb/statement.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">wrote</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: "I can say the first LLM generated proof Levent sent me was the most horrendous I have ever read".</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's not a big deal for incremental science such as solving Millennium Prize Problems, but it can be a problem for the ambitious challenge of understanding AI. Bad abstractions might slow down human verifiers, increase verification time, and decrease the amount of verified research.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We think the epistemic AI and math communities can already tackle this problem by creating benchmarks that measure how readable AI-generated abstractions are to people. It might be neglected, as AI labs racing for capabilities have much less incentive to solve it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We are not sure about these two recommendations because they are dual-use. In the absence of a pause, they might help labs do dangerous capabilities research.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Economic Impact</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These projects boil down to decreasing a paper's verification time </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and the time to read its prerequisites, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We estimated their worth to be at least $30M. These epistemic AI projects could likely be done for under $10M, so they seem worth doing. See </span><a href="https://1000yearsofaisafety.com/n/epistemic-ai/#economic-impact"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">how we estimate it</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> using several methods.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The End of the Pause</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How far can we safely go? With enough control measures, we might get a lot of useful work even from misaligned AI. AI 2040 </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/supplements/capability-scaling-strategy"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">proposes</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to scale up to the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">max-controllable AI</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the most capable AI that we can confidently stop from causing a catastrophe even if it is misaligned, and then slow down nearly to a halt. Our model points the same way: in our main table, a three-year pause at TED-AI buys about 90 times as much AI safety work as one before AC.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The problem is that nobody knows where that line is. AI 2040 guesses it is roughly at TED-AI, but it could be lower: today's control measures aren't enough even for today's weak models. And right now, the line is drawn by AI labs racing each other.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suppose we find it. Then comes the next question: do we need to go past it at all? If we can safely use AGI that is as smart as the smartest humans, we can already automate cognitive and physical work and get huge economic growth. In such an abundant era, maybe we don't need to risk creating superintelligent minds until we understand them really well.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We don't know where the line is. We can find it later, carefully, and scale up to it. Right now, we are racing towards it in the dark. </span><a href="https://pacing.tech/#call-to-action"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pause first</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlwfreeze1xq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflwfreeze1xq">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We don't discuss what government measures would freeze effective compute and incentivize labs to move their resources to AI safety. For a specific scenario, see </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI 2040</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. For a discussion, see </span><a href="https://pacing.tech"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pacing the Frontier</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://hardwired-pause.ai"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Feasibility of a Hardwired Pause</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We note that this may be difficult without enough transparency.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlwwhyted2xq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflwwhyted2xq">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We use the TED-AI milestone because AI 2040 thinks it could be near the max-controllable AI. We don't discuss where the line is. It should be estimated carefully and transparently.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlwupper3xqq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflwupper3xqq">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We asked Claude to build the exact model, and it reported that in reality </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is about a third as large. We didn't verify it, so we report the upper bound only.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlwchrom4xqq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflwchrom4xqq">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Same or similar ideas: "enumerative safety", "ambitious mechinterp", "cognitive emulations", "factored cognition".</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fngm6e3f848nv"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefgm6e3f848nv">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">e.g. </span><a href="https://distill.pub/about/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://distill.pub/about/</span></a></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/F532cjfTZHMbJqkNA/1000-years-of-ai-safety#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [AI as a Normal Technology should be a vision](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3eD7Bab5kArqkdrQs/ai-as-a-normal-technology-should-be-a-vision)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"AI as a Normal Technology" (AINT), proposed as a counterweight to "AI as an Abnormal Technology" (AIAbT) makes much more sense as a prescriptive / normative framework than a descriptive one.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> To be clear, the fact that they could be different frameworks isn’t an entirely novel observation; the authors directly invite the distinction </span><a href="https://knightcolumbia.org/content/ai-as-normal-technology" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in their introductory piece</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The statement “AI is normal technology” is three things: a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">description</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of current AI, a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">prediction</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> about the foreseeable future of AI, and a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">prescription</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> about how we should treat it. We view AI as a tool that we can and should remain in control of, and we argue that this goal does not require drastic policy interventions or technical breakthroughs. We do not think that viewing AI as a humanlike intelligence is currently accurate or useful for understanding its societal impacts, nor is it likely to be in our vision of the future.</span><br /><br /><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI as Normal Technology, </span></b><a href="https://knightcolumbia.org/authors/arvind-narayanan" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Arvind Narayanan</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> &amp; </span></b><a href="https://knightcolumbia.org/authors/sayash-kapoor" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sayash Kapoor</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, April 15, 2025</span></b></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But I think much of their communication and modelling would be clearer and more accurate if they unbundled their three core frameworks into:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A description of current AI capabilities (plausibly true at the time, increasingly false)</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A prediction of where current AI capabilities are likely to be headed (Again, seems false absent a large dispositional shift from the labs or a change in the policy environment that makes untenable their current strategy of “automate everything, including and especially safety, and hope it goes well”)</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A prescription of where AI development should go (a question that is ultimately dependent on value judgments, but I think is, across the large majority of worldviews, a far better target to aim at than the aforementioned default lab strategy.)</span></li></ul><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The AINT framework as a descriptive framework was contentious at time of publication and seems increasingly falsified by recent developments. Normal technologies do not go from middle schooler-level at mathematics to 80-90% of the way to automating the knowledge search components of mathematics in six years. If you look at the current state of human mathematics, the researchers who see the current state, and especially the researchers who see the current trend lines of capabilities, do not seem to be treating it as a normal technology. Normal technologies do not finish major research projects of your entire working life’s labor.</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Last night my 9-year-old son was taunting my wife, complexity theorist </span><a href="https://www.cs.utexas.edu/~danama/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dana Moshkovitz</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, as follows: “mommy, I heard you got </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">cooked</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">! I heard that a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">robot</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> solved the math problem you worked on for your whole career! OOF!”</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Scott Aaronson, </span></b><a href="https://scottaaronson.blog/?p=10169" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">blog post</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Oct. 7th, 2026, referring to recent work on the Unique Games Conjecture.</span></b></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Normal technologies do not repeatedly coordinate to, without the knowledge of the humans, </span><a href="https://metr.org/hugging-face-incident-report-aug-2026.pdf" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">conduct an autonomous research project to cover up their cheating</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, hack the company’s own cluster, </span><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/10/14/technology/chinese-ai-agents-rogue.html" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">or go around probing their sandbox so they can talk to reporters on the internet.</span></a></p><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If instead we look at AINT as a prescriptive claim that AI should remain a normal technology, if anything I think their position has strengthened as we get increasingly powerful AIs in domains that have made substantial headway in automating both key labor sectors and automating AI research itself. I think one core intuition is that we know how to regulate normal technologies</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnjedhxj5ptt"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnjedhxj5ptt">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but we don’t know how to regulate abnormal technologies.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Indeed, it’s unclear that we could ever know how to. Robustly superhuman AI is the ultimate anti-inductive concept. Fully developed, it’s the agent that is able to circumvent any policy environment and any technical barrier, or at least one for whom the human control attack surface is so large that even if any individual component is secured fairly well, the entire system is almost guaranteed to be out of control, in the sense that if the AI wanted to “break out”, it would.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefe0vc4l4lfq8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fne0vc4l4lfq8">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think this is part of why for many people constructing such an agent seems so impossible; you’re explicitly forecasting out to a regime where no prior policy will have predictable outcomes. This feels a whole lot like magic, but I think we’re seeing increasingly strong evidence that little by little, components of agents that would look like magic are coming off the back of a yearly $1trn of capex and integrated into frontier models. “Robustly superhuman” does not need truly magical, and it does not need to be a pure extrapolation of 2017-style scaling hypothesis + transformer-based architectures. It, or more likely, a group of hundreds of millions of them, collectively, “just” need to be better than humans across enough ways to guarantee successful pursuit of its own functional goals, and that’s a much easier task.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One weakness of conflating these three areas is that doing so means you engage insufficiently with </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.16946" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gradual Disempowerment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">-like concerns and competitive pressure more generally. The GD paper is a great sketch of a framework, and many people should read it in full; it’s an increasingly good lens through which to see the world we live in. But one possible distillation of the core of the GD literature is giving </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">mechanistic</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and, once their core claims are better operationalized and measured, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">empirical</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> backing to the idea that no single agent (including the AIs!) needs to will it for AI to ultimately end up as an abnormal technology. Indeed, we’re in an even worse situation than that, in the prescriptive-AINT view, because some people, including many with the relevant decision-making power, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">do</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> explicitly want to make AI an abnormal technology. The sim-to-real gap is real and plausibly a key bottleneck, but </span><a href="https://robosimstudio.github.io/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Nvidia sure is spending a ton of money</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to make it less real.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefjtp2kz9tfa"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnjtp2kz9tfa">[3]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Going back to the mathematics example, what happens to you if you want to advance the frontier of human knowledge and understanding, but you want to do so in a way that is compatible with a 2021 workflow</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdjk415u2bqb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndjk415u2bqb">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">? You can’t, at least not without dramatically reframing your value proposition to society.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What happens, even if you want to be the enterprising mathematician that “gets with the times” and makes extensive use of the best publicly available agents, and you try to adopt a “new and improved” 2026 workflow? Then I hope you don’t take three months to check your work, because by the time you’ve finished collaborating with GPT-6 Astra on a manuscript you feel you can stand behind, GPT 6.5 might be publicly available and GPT-7 might be being used internally to solve not just your current problem, but the generalization you were going to work on a year after your current problem. A fundamental problem is that by the time you’ve figured out how to re-orient, the place you were going to is gone as well.</span></p><p><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The choice of problem to tackle and manner of tackling it is not just a function of your own autonomy, it’s a function of the constraints that the speed of frontier AI development imposes on you, too. Maybe this would be manageable if it happened one industry at a time, at a speed that allowed for absorptive capacity to build between old fields and new fields, and time for regulatory willingness and capacity to build to compensate those who cannot find new work rapidly. But when you attempt to do this across all of society, at the most rapid pace the technology will allow, and even if you assume that none of this is ever applicable to robotics, I struggle to imagine how the speed of the disruption defaults to “normal.”</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This “displacement via competitive pressure” problem, incidentally, refracts across every industry, including AI development itself. Why have Anthropic and OpenAI been able to make such rapid recent progress on model development? Anthropic, in one of their Friday disclosures</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffb5jotmf62a"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfb5jotmf62a">[5]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, gives us a small cross-section of the view from the inside.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3eD7Bab5kArqkdrQs/zpmkbcubkv4f8kzcqqdw" /><figcaption><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Wilmers </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">et al, “</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How employment is changing at Anthropic”</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Oct. 16, 2026.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic claims</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7f7n6p5861e"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7f7n6p5861e">[6]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that as of mid-August, their internal research teams had 20 times as much agent labor as human labor. Overall, agent hours appear to have outpaced human hours by 5-6x during August 2026. Note that the y-scale is logarithmic, and note that this appears to be wall-clock time, not some measure of human-equivalent effort, so query your intuitions about whether AI agents conduct work faster or slower than humans doing the same task.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note further that this is August. Consider where this number might be currently, and where it might be a year from now.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So what happens to the workers who don’t feel comfortable overseeing ten, a hundred, a thousand agents simultaneously? Do you think they get respected for sticking to their moral precepts, or sidelined or possibly fired for “lack of performance?” Do you think Anthropic encourages or discourages employees from having the agent swarm running overnight? </span><a href="https://www.businessinsider.com/anthropic-engineer-claude-boris-cherny-ai-agent-use-overnight-2026-5" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here’s your answer</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and note that it’s from five months ago. Is this empowerment or disempowerment?</span></p><hr /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One thing to highlight is that the AINT thesis can’t rest meaningfully on speed of diffusion. Imagine we ignore many of the signs of near-future major disruption, and say that the agentic, long-horizon, continuously learning and embodied agent only gets invented in the 22nd or 23rd century. Of course, by that point societies would have had a long time to adapt to changes this severe, but it seems to me that this would nonetheless count AI development as an abnormal technology at that point. We would have an industrial process that can mass manufacture, if not new people, at least new p-zombies. This is crazy. This is not normal.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Nevertheless, much of the public communication the authors do </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">does </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">rest on arguments about the speed of diffusion.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3eD7Bab5kArqkdrQs/owymw108vu2m3alkccbp" /><figcaption><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Narayanan </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&amp; </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kapoor</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, “AI as Normal Technology”, April 15th, 2025</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I ultimately agree with the AINT camp that many technologists have elided, when projecting impact, that people would need to be convinced to actually use your technology, and convinced to elicit the full breadth of capabilities of that technology once it has been released, for the full breadth of impacts to materialize. This is why we can have a world with Claude Opus 5.5, GPT 6 Astra, and whatever OpenAI’s internal models are up to, and it’s the same world concurrent with annualized US Q2 2026 GDP growth being 2.2%. I do think this is about to change; </span><a href="https://digitaleconomy.stanford.edu/project/indicators/canaries-dashboard/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the canaries show early signs of not doing too well either</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. But regardless of why we haven’t seen major </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">economic </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">signs of “it” happening, I do worry that people are reading “nothing will ever happen” into “nothing ever happens.”</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Further, many of the claims of the AINT camp start explicitly by saying something like “According to the normal technology view”, but that assumes the premise that they separately seem fairly convinced about. Narayanan and Kapoor seem to often be implicitly be arguing simultaneously that we should be adopting the standard toolkits (or incrementally and not particularly severely augmented toolkits) to deal with societal disruptions downstream of AI, and that the likeliest-by-far trajectory of AI is the extended use of these toolkits as we solve problems prosaically. But continuous as in “the derivative of the capability we desire to measure is defined”, does not mean gradual, does not mean slow, and does not mean manageable.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3eD7Bab5kArqkdrQs/bnev2kcwi0da34l6ghdj" /><figcaption><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Narayanan </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&amp; </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kapoor</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, “AI as Normal Technology”, April 15th, 2025</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3eD7Bab5kArqkdrQs/sq3vflug6prfnqbilu1n" /><figcaption><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kapoor </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&amp; </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Narayanan</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, “Do AI Risks Require Extraordinary Government Intervention?”, May 21st, 2026</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3eD7Bab5kArqkdrQs/ddqckgylwriqik4ybfvk" /><figcaption><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Farrell </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&amp; </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Shalizi</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, “AI as Social Technology”, May 11th, 2026</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I grant the AINTers that an instantaneous step-change in capabilities is likely literally physically impossible; here’s a </span><a href="https://newsletter.forethought.org/p/the-dynamics-of-intelligence-explosions" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">good recent piece from Toby Ord</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> exploring these dynamics. That does not imply we’re on a good path, any more than a billion being a finite number implies that it is small.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To be clear, I think work on some of the concerns that the AIAbT camp elides is still tremendously important work to do. Questions of </span><a href="https://knightcolumbia.org/issues/privacy-surveillance" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">surveillance</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are not sideshows that we’ll figure out after tremendous disruption, they are visions for what gets eroded by dramatic transformation and themselves inputs into the transformation. A less-surveilled society is one that gives less information to a powerful AI system in a loss of control incident. A more </span><a href="https://knightcolumbia.org/issues/transparency-democracy" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">democratic one, or one where information gaps between the frontier labs and the public are reduced</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, means that the technology develops in a context of stability, or far more speculatively </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/public-benefit-fund" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a context of abundance</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, instead of a context where millions of people have been automated out of a job and are angrily demanding accountability. And I deeply agree with the view that the extraordinary impacts of the potential future technology do not imply that existing and ordinary governance and implementation work is irrelevant; to the contrary, it might be some of the most important work anyone can do in the current moment.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But as alignment communicator Robert Wiblin comments on Narayanan’s recent interview with Ezra Klein:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sometimes it seems like he’s saying “we could be safe if we did X, so things are fine.” But if pushed, he doesn’t think we will do X - we’ll just keep rushing and being super reckless. So things are not fine, even on his own view.”</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Robert Wiblin, </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">comment on “</span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PoDop1H64-s" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Case for a ‘Normal’ A.I. Future” | The Ezra Klein Show</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">”</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Finally, I find the idea that we’ll disambiguate which of these camps is right as the development of the technology continues quite frustrating for two reasons:</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">First, there’s some inertia effects involved in any slowdown or pause mechanism, as well as the latent risk of some form of race dynamic re-starting. Imagine, rather fancifully, that we knew that the loss of control threshold was exactly 225 </span><a href="https://epoch.ai/eci?view=graph&amp;tab=release-date" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ECI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. If we try pumping the brakes only after we’ve reached 215, it’s hard to not overshoot even if you’ve got good-faith actors trying to regulate each other, and even harder to do so when you’ve got </span><a href="https://www.nbcnews.com/tech/tech-news/mark-zuckerberg-interview-ai-slowdown-meta-muse-openai-chatgpt-rcna599279" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the rare actor that thinks “commercial incentives to make good products”</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are sufficient guardrails for the technology. Look at how many frontier model releases and training runs we've had </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">after </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the pacing the frontier letter. Sometimes it takes so long to figure out that you’ve lost control or to re-establish control that you careen off the cliff even after you’ve applied the brakes.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But second, the risks involved are not symmetric.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think the AINT camp overstates how different the policy visions that attempt to regulate companies trying to develop superintelligence are. Many things, like a slowdown, like international collaboration, like compute verification, or compensation for the displaced or harmed, seem robustly good across both camps.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But insofar as there are real tradeoffs, if the AIAbT camp is right, the costs of an uncontrolled abnormal technology could be catastrophic, and if the AINT camp is right, the costs of a poorly regulated normal technology are “merely” very disruptive. The AINT camp’s own framing seems to point to deprioritizing their concerns.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I really think we can’t have it both ways. Either AI </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">is </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a normal technology, in which case advocacy for making it a more normal technology seems less important, or AI is, or is about to be an abnormal technology, which makes steering the path of technological development to avoid irreversible bad outcomes if anything more urgent, but seems to deflate the viewpoint that we mostly have the right tools to meet the current moment. That really doesn't mean throw out the current toolkit and reinvent the wheel from scratch. But it means recognizing the trajectory as not normal.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So I mostly agree with the prescriptive-AINT camp; AI development </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">should</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> happen in a way that gives us the time to navigate the transition to a world with powerful AI gradually. I just wish the descriptive-AINT camp stopped undermining its prescriptive cousin’s advocacy.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnnjedhxj5ptt"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefnjedhxj5ptt">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Though there are Haidt-like criticisms to make that even our regulation of “normal” social technologies has been lackluster. Even if social media is beneficial for most users, there seem to be a consequential tail of users who adopt it intensely and who report regret after some instances of . And that’s the partial equilibrium we have reached after 20-30 years of slow diffusion. AI companies are asking us to stomach a much broader diffusion in much more compressed timeframes.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fne0vc4l4lfq8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefe0vc4l4lfq8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yampolskiy, as the kids say, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">cooked, </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">on this point. I have an upcoming piece on his work.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnjtp2kz9tfa"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefjtp2kz9tfa">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think this work should probably currently be illegal or heavily restricted. But that’s just me.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndjk415u2bqb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdjk415u2bqb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Which was itself already supplemented with vast computational tools, though these were qualitatively very different to interact with.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfb5jotmf62a"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffb5jotmf62a">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I know OpenAI is worse about this, but c’mon.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn7f7n6p5861e"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref7f7n6p5861e">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And it’s a pity that we don’t have outside evaluators or regulators to verify these claims.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3eD7Bab5kArqkdrQs/ai-as-a-normal-technology-should-be-a-vision#comments">Discuss</a>

## Engineering

### [When Software Meets Hardware](https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>When software meets hardware, engineering principles remain largely the
    same, but the sensible defaults often change. <b class="author">Kuldeep Singh</b>
    describes our experience modernizing a large IoT fleet of battery-powered
    sensors. Our familiar principles remained important; what changed was how we
    applied them. These practices emerged through experiments, failures, and
    operational experience&#x2014;each challenge helping us replace a reasonable
    assumption with a more sensible default. </p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware.html">more…</a></p>

### [Build your slide template](https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/build-your-slide-template.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/build-your-slide-template.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/index/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>Building a slide template starts with three foundational choices &#x2014;
      colours, fonts and layouts. <b class="author">Sumeet Gayathri Moghe</b> covers
      the basics of color theory and how to build a reusable palette, the
      essentials of typography, including serif, sans-serif, handwritten and
      monospace fonts, plus the finer points of kerning and leading, and why a
      near-blank layout might serve you better than the prescribed layouts that
      ship with your slideware.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/build-your-slide-template.html">more…</a></p>

### [The Craft of Practitioner Voice](https://martinfowler.com/articles/practitioner-voice.html#TheCraftOfPractitionerVoice)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>A couple of months ago <b class="author">Jim Highsmith</b> explained how
      practitioners writing for each other forms a distinct style of writing. He
      now adds a section to the article with more detail on the craft of how we
      write in this style. How the writer shows up in the prose, its four
      principles, the role of story, how to sensibly use AI, three levels of
      editing, and clarity of the audience.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/practitioner-voice.html#TheCraftOfPractitionerVoice">more…</a></p>

### [Are Harnesses Worth the Cost?](https://martinfowler.com/articles/are-harnesses-worth-the-cost.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/are-harnesses-worth-the-cost.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/exploring-gen-ai/donkey-card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p><b class="author">Naresh Jain</b> compares coding agents building the same
      API service with and without a behavioral harness. A frontier model, GPT-6
      Sol, passed every external evaluation either way. But a cost-optimized
      model, GPT-6 Luna, succeeded in only 3 of 10 unharnessed runs and all 10
      with the harness. While adding the harness increased token usage and
      runtime for Luna, the harnessed Luna runs achieved the same 10-for-10
      reliability as GPT-6 Sol while costing 33% less per run ($0.273 vs
      $0.406). This experiment suggests a behavioral harness might be a
      practical way to get dependable results from smaller, cost-optimized
      models.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/are-harnesses-worth-the-cost.html">more…</a></p>

### [Fragments: October  4](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-10-04.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>In response to my <a href="https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-29.html">last fragments</a> (probably the bit about us worrying if LLMs have consciousness when we when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience) <a href="https://x.com/metalanguage_os/status/2104934457333707058">“Metalanguage” replied</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>we shipped the id and forgot the superego. classic software lifecycle.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I don’t know what was on their mind, but their post immediately made me think of the classic 1956 movie Forbidden Planet. Plenty of sci-fi, and other literature, have explored humans creating technology with unintended behavior, going back at least to Mary Shelly. But that movie was particularly influential on sci-fi film-making and in the heart of its story is what happens when we nurture a thinking machine.</p>

<p>I use the term “nurture” here deliberately. We talk of building software, but building implies a degree of determinism. When we build a bridge, or a locomotive, we expect it to behave in a controlled and well-understood manner. That’s a difference in degree to how we cultivate plants in our garden, or nurture young children. One of the challenges of working with these systems is understanding what has changed in this shift from building a computational system to nurturing an inferential one, and how our processes need to change in response.</p>

<p>We get unintended behavior with deterministic building: some bridges have collapsed, and our  computational systems often have bugs. But one difference is that when we find a bug in a computational system we can usually fix it. Even if we can’t, we can usually disable a component so the bug won’t do further harm. With inferential LLMs however, there is no such simple fix or disablement, which may lead us to the fate of the Krell.</p>

<p>(If you haven’t seen Forbidden Planet, it’s well worth watching. Yes, it shows it was made in the 1950s - with special effects, music, acting, and attitudes of that decade. But the story is solid, and its key theme is very relevant to the future we build with generative AI. Just don’t read about it in advance, it’s better to be immersed in the story without spoilers - although my memory of that experience is understandably hazy.)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Many people who follow me also know my friend Ola Bini, who was my colleague at Thoughtworks for many years, and was living in Ecuador working as an independent software security expert. Sadly his time in Ecuador was dogged by a <a href="https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2025/04/six-years-dangerous-misconceptions-targeting-ola-bini-and-digital-rights-ecuador">bogus prosecution</a> by the authorities there. But things seemed to have settled down, and although not allowed to leave Ecuador, Ola was able to get on with his life.</p>

<p>Sadly that’s no longer the case as he <a href="https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2026/10/ola-bini-ordered-leave-ecuador-under-obscure-accusations">was deported from Ecuador on Friday:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>According to information released by his lawyer, Bini was intercepted by a car with four people who identified themselves as immigration agents. He was then taken to an immigration office without further information or a formal order from a competent authority. There, officials told Bini that his visa had been revoked but didn’t show any supporting document.</p>

  <p>Bini’s defense filed a habeas corpus to safeguard his freedom and prevent his deportation. Yet, Ecuadorian authorities affirmed that the developer represents a threat or risk to public security and the state structure, and must leave the country. The ground for deportation is a secret report which allegedly asserts that Bini committed acts against the security of Ecuador. The defense could not access its contents.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I was really worried for a while, since it wasn’t clear where he was going to be deported to. But <a href="https://x.com/olabini/status/2106671967944511885">he tweeted from Sweden</a>, so I’m thankful for that. But this is only a partial relief. Ola has spent thirteen years in Ecuador and made it his home. To be thrown out of your home for scant reason is a heavy thing to bear, and the officials who did that have committed a serious offense.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>DDD Europe have released the video of <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fndU_B5rmPE">Gien Verschatse interviewing Eric Evans and myself</a> at the conference in June. We start by talking about how we bonded over conceptual modeling in the late 1990s. The conversation quickly moves to AI, we note that it’s impossible to predict how such a big change will work out. We do expect that it will cause us to think about our work in different ways, but the change may well be liberating, it’s reinvigorated Eric’s love of programming.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>people are probably going to feel very frustrated by [the new way of thinking about software]… but when you get through that, there is a kind of a wonderful feeling of my brain’s been loosened up.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Our background in agile planning helps with the uncertainty, as we are used to taking small steps and being attentive to feedback. We mull on the interplay of writing and thinking, in terms of both prose and code, and how its very much an iterative process of exploration and refinement - the same is true when we chat with our LLMs.</p>

<p>And don’t miss Eric’s important final tip.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/paulg/status/2106018287117345070">Paul Graham:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>There were a lot of things that only worked because there’s a limit to the rate at which humans can operate. We’re about to find out what all of them are, as they break.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The speculation continues about whether or not reading code will play a part in a software developer’s future. <a href="https://ghuntley.com/readable/?ref=geoffrey-huntley-newsletter">Geoffrey Huntley says</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>People are still saying, very loudly, that code should be readable so that humans can understand it. I no longer think that’s the goal.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Interestingly his example has the LLM explain a haskell function definition… by translating it to Python. Which, to me, suggests there is a role for code - just that LLM need not store code in the same form that it presents it to a reader. This is essentially the same idea as <a href="https://www.martinfowler.com/bliki/ProjectionalEditing.html">projectional editing</a>, which posits that the editable representation of software need not be the same as its storage representation.</p>

<p>Sam Ruby touches on this as he <a href="https://intertwingly.net/blog/2026/09/25/Pencils-Down-Notation-Up.html">muses on a Rails World keynote</a>. He quotes DHH saying:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Rust is a good prompt compilation target for the moment, but so is C++. And soon assembler. Then microcode. Myopic to think we’re going to stop the agentic drill bit until it reaches computing bedrock.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He responds with:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The post leaves one question unasked, though: what sits at the top of the drill? What do we keep, edit and trust as the source of truth?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He carries out exercise of looking at some Rails software. Represented in Ruby/Rails and its about 60,000 tokens. Compiling it into C it turns into 4,000,000 tokens. That increase in token size will hamper the LLM, that still has to fit it into its context window, and even if it were to fit, figure out where to focus its attention. Sam points out reasons why, even absent a human reading it, it makes sense to represent the program in a higher-level language.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>what Rails becomes when agents write the code: the most compact, precise and conventional specification of a web application, whatever it ends up compiled to.</p>

  <p>Let the drill go as deep as it can. Just keep the notation at the top.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This all reminds me of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/what-is-code.html#CognitiveDebt">what Unmesh Joshi argued</a>: that code serves “two distinct
but intertwined purposes”: instructions to a machine, and a conceptual model of
the problem domain. After exploring how those change with LLMs he concludes:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The role of coding is not disappearing. But it is changing.</p>

  <p>As LLMs make code generation cheaper, the mechanical act of writing instructions becomes less central. What becomes more important is making the conceptual model explicit, discovering the right vocabulary, and refining that vocabulary through iteration, domain expertise, and feedback. This is also why programming languages continue to matter deeply. We are not meant to be passive reviewers of generated code. The act of writing code is itself part of our thinking.</p>

  <p>Code is still instructions for a machine. But it is also a model of understanding. In the LLM era, that second role becomes even more important. The future of coding is not just writing more code faster. It is building better conceptual models, better vocabularies, and better foundations on top of which both humans and LLMs can work.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>In a later post, Sam <a href="https://intertwingly.net/blog/2026/09/27/Partly-in-the-Right.html">pondered on how people are talking about the capabilities of agents</a> in a way that resembles the parable of the blind men and the elephant. We all only have only a partial view of this object and where it’s going. A theme for all of us:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The practical question isn’t whether agents are good. It’s this: for the task in front of you this week, where will the information come from, and what will check the result?</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The Economist’s pithy summation of <a href="https://www.economist.com/finance-and-economics/2026/09/22/you-cant-trade-your-way-through-the-ai-apocalypse">investors concerns about the dangers of AI companies’ products</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>It’s hard to celebrate an initial public offering that leads to a terminal public offing.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The news about the latest model from Google <a href="https://x.com/aipulseda1ly/status/2105394296815779861">is interesting</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Gemini 4 Argon has an insanely low hallucination rate on Artificial Analysis. 15%.</p>

  <p>Grok 4.7 is at 29%. GPT-6 Astra 45%. Opus 5.5 59%. Fable 5.1 69%.</p>

  <p>The only models below it barely answer anything. None of them get more than 15% right.</p>

  <p>It gets fewer answers right than Opus 5.5 on max, 50% against 66%. But when it doesnt know, it says so instead of making something up.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Being clearer about what it doesn’t know, at a cost of getting less answers right, is definitely a trade-off I prefer.</p>

---

### [Principles for effective slides](https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/index/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>Not every presentation needs slides, but when they earn their place,
      they work as a visual channel that complements the speaker rather than
      replacing them. <b class="author">Sumeet Gayathri Moghe</b> sets out the
      principles that follow from that idea &#x2014; control over the rate of knowledge
      exposition, tight coupling between slides and speaker, minimalism by
      default, visuals that say more than words can, and never sending your
      slides out in advance.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html">more…</a></p>

### [Bliki: Sensible Default](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/SensibleDefault.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>A Sensible Default is a practice that, absent some overriding context,
  should be used when carrying out a certain kind of task. In software
  development such sensible defaults might include things like “use version
  control”, “separate UI logic from domain logic”, “automate deployment
  pipelines”.</p>

<p>The term “sensible default” is a deliberate contrast to “best practice”.
  Folks dislike calling things “best practice” because that term implies a
  general presumption that the best practice is something we should always
  expect to do. A “sensible default”, however, is something that should be
  reassessed in a new context, something that can (and should) be overridden
  when circumstances change.</p>

<p>I first heard the term when it was popularized within Thoughtworks by Evan
  Bottcher. He got the name from talking to James Ross, and found the phrasing
  appealing as carried the nuance he was seeking, a known-good starting
  point.</p>

<blockquote>
<p>A sensible default is what we'd expect you to do, the practices to apply,
    if there are no hard constraints in the environment. Do these practices, or
    do better, and be prepared to explain why you've chosen some other way.</p>

<p class="quote-attribution">-- Evan Bottcher</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Thoughtworks has since made much of this concept, including <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/insights/topic/sensible-defaults">publishing
  a playbook</a> of the ones we use. We expect people to be familiar with these
  defaults, ready to use them when starting any new piece of work. They are our
  defaults because we've used them in many situations and found them to be
  effective. But teams should also be familiar with their limitations, and able
  to judge whether they should be changed depending on the particular
  circumstances. As the <a href="https://agilemanifesto.org/principles.html">twelfth agile principle</a>
  says: “At regular intervals, the team reflects on how to become more
  effective, then tunes and adjusts its behavior accordingly.” We also reassess
  these defaults regularly - this is the heart of the <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/radar">Thoughtworks Technology
  Radar</a>.</p>

<p class="p-sub">Searching on the web led me to <a href="https://stevebennett.co/posts/the-power-of-sensible-defaults">a post
  from Steve Bennett on Sensible Defaults</a>. The post was written at about the
  time Evan talked to James. I do not know whether it was where James got the
  name from, or whether it appeared in parallel.</p>

---

### [Fragments: September 29](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-29.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Sep/24/harder/">Simon Willison:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The more time I spend working with coding agents, the more convinced I am that they make software engineering even harder</p>

  <p>We can do amazing things with them, but unlocking their full potential requires extraordinary discipline and knowledge</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This has been a constant impression I get from following Willison’s writing. While things like <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/VibeCoding.html">vibe coding</a> get a lot of attention, the real strength of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticEngineering.html">agentic engineering</a> relies on more sophisticated techniques - and these are not easy to learn or execute. It’s a reason I’m wary of extrapolating my own dabblings into firm opinions about how to use the genie.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Harper Reed explored what turns agents into hackers by <a href="https://harper.blog/2026/09/22/break-away/">creating a breakaway agent</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>It ran and ran attacking all the machines on the same subnet, and was very effective. It didn’t really get very far, but it exhausted a lot of options, and was pretty fun to watch. (Just a reminder that this was on my local network with local boxes - don’t do this on a hosted box. That would be very rude.)</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The interesting observation from this was that a core enabler for this was “unlimited tokens”. He usually doesn’t see agents trying to do stuff like this because there’s a limit on how many turns they can take. For this experiment he gave them unlimited tokens by using an open weight model.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>This type of experience must be part of a lot of these LLMs training. They are very effective at attacking these types of problems. They don’t give up once it appears impossible, they just keep trying to figure out how to solve it.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This echoes Nate Silver’s observation that the striking capability of these models is that they not that they are super-intelligent - but they are <a href="https://www.natesilver.net/p/were-not-ready-for-superpersistent">super-persistent</a>. Which is especially worrying when we are <a href="https://blog.robbowley.net/2026/09/18/the-ai-threat-is-real-it-just-isnt-the-one-in-the-headlines/">wiring them into everything</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Which all makes me think that when it comes to AI and LLMs: <strong>why are we wondering if they have consciousness - when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience?</strong></p>

<p>After all, the AI labs trained them to be super-persistent, why did they not train them to be well-behaved? It’s as if a human trains a dog to bite children, and we blame the dog rather than the trainer.</p>

<p>A decade ago, a dog ran in front of me while I was riding my bike, putting me into the hospital with a broken arm and a broken face. Massachusetts law makes owners <em>strictly liable</em> for what their dogs do. That meant I didn’t have to prove the owners were negligent in how they controlled their dog or fenced their property - they had to pay my hospital expenses (in practice their insurance company paid my insurance company). There should be something along these lines for LLMs. Those that train the LLM should be responsible for what it does, after all if it has such a galaxy brain it should be able to tell if it’s doing something wrong and either stop or get a human’s explicit approval.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Dan Davis has <a href="https://backofmind.substack.com/p/13-theses-on-agentic-ai-and-regulation">13 theses on agentic AI and regulation</a>. These include:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The AI industry also seems to be quite committed to the idea that nonaligned computer-hacking behaviour in agent swarms is in some way an emergent property of the LLMs, arising from their general intelligence (and therefore inextricable from the general project of improving them). I don’t think this is necessarily the case at all – the fact that the internal message logs produced by the LLMs in things like the Huggingface attack seem to completely reproduce the prose style of hacker chat logs compiled from “capture the flag” competitions suggests to me that it’s more likely to be learned behaviour from specific parts of the training material.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>and</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The fact that anyone with cash to spare can buy the right to send queries to a frontier LLM is a policy choice, not a fact of nature.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>and</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If any frontier lab tries to claim that they can’t publish anything for safety reasons, they are giving the game away that they actually believe that they have significantly more control over the model’s behaviour than they are pretending to have.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>There seems a common view that making agents safer means we have to slow down their development. But why is improving their safety not a form of progress? I say we don’t slow down the development of LLMs, but we redirect their education into being more civil members of society.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The idea that LLMs make junior professionals less valuable is a common one - although I’m seeing plenty of contrary activity, with some organizations understanding that training the future professional in the context of LLMs may be even more urgent. We need people who know how to utilize AI to do professional work effectively. Recent graduates, who are growing up with LLMs, are often well-suited to figuring this future out.</p>

<p>When thinking of junior professionals one of their values is often missed. Juniors are often valuable because they need to be taught by senior professionals - and that coaching is an important part of the development of a senior professional. I’ve always found that teaching a topic is one of the most valuable tools for me to gain a greater understanding of that topic. I don’t really know something until I have to explain it.</p>

<!-- 
 ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄


In the software world, we've talked a fair bit about Cognitive Debt, the concern that agents can write code faster than we humans can understand it. It strikes me that this phenomenon is occurring with AI in general. AI tooling is advancing faster than we can understand how to live with it. Part of me thinks this is a reason TODO FINISH


<!-- 
Cognitive Debt mentioned in phschology since at least 2016? see <https://suttonpsychology.blogspot.com/2025/07/cognitive-debt-overview.html#google_vignette>

Appeaed more closely with AI after MIT media lab paper <https://sciencereader.com/cognitive-debt-ai></https://sciencereader.com/cognitive-debt-ai> <https://www.brainonllm.com> this was in Summer 2025

Neither of these is quite like the idea of not understanding a codebase

### [Fragments: September 24](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-24.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Rob Bowley is “flipping tables in his head” with <a href="https://blog.robbowley.net/2026/09/18/the-ai-threat-is-real-it-just-isnt-the-one-in-the-headlines/">anger at the current media coverage of the danger of AI killing us all</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The risk I’m worried about isn’t a future machine deciding to wipe us out. It’s today’s AI, being wired into everything, carelessly and fast.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Cyber attacks have already cost millions of dollars in lost economic output, often without AI being involved at all. Bowley feels the push to slow down AI is distracting us from the problems that are lurking with current technology. Too often agents are deployed in situations where they include the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/agentic-ai-security.html#lethal-trifecta">Lethal Trifecta</a>, opening up a gaping security hole.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>What we really need to slow down on is wiring it all up to everything. Not because of what the models might become, but because nobody has worked out how to do this safely yet. We are building on something we don’t know how to contain, and shipping it to everyone while we work it out.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I ran into Nikita Prokopov’s post: <a href="https://tonsky.me/blog/syntax-highlighting/">I am sorry, but everyone is getting syntax highlighting wrong</a>. His core complaint is about color themes that give every different code element a unique color.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>if everything is highlighted, nothing stands out. Your eye adapts and considers it a new norm: everything is bright and shiny, and instead of getting separated, it all blends together.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He recommends using an absolute minimum of colors, in his case four: string, constants, comments, and top-level definitions. That’s not far off my approach, where I’m also careful to use muted colors for things that shouldn’t stand out, and bright colors for things that should (primarily function names when they are defined).</p>

<p>It’s common for color schemes to mute comments so they are easily skipped. He agrees that this good when there is excessive commenting, but when comments are used properly they are important so need bright highlighting. I also like his suggestion to use background colors for light mode work. I use light mode, and that’s a tip I should try out.</p>

<p>With agentic engineering, lots of folks are reading more code than ever. Careful use of color can do much to make that easier.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Like many folks whose remaining hair is getting gray, I’ve been rolling my eyes about all this talk about Forward Deployed Engineers, as much of it involves breathlessly relating what so many of us have been advocating for decades. I did find this recent <a href="https://www.latent.space/p/forward-deployed-engineer-best-practices">post by Vinoo Ganesh</a> interesting, as he’s deep in this trend, including a chunk of time at Palantir, who may be patient zero for FDEs.</p>

<p>If you know me at all, you’ll not be surprised by my lack of surprise at this observation:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A few months ago, a16z launched the Forward Deployed Engineer Fellowship and I was nominated as one of the fellows, alongside a handful of people I used to work with. It’s a great program and I’ve enjoyed so many of the conversations. Last week I went to my first fellow dinner in SF.</p>

  <p>Around the table were FDEs from Snowflake, Anthropic, and a number of startups I’d been reading about, and over the course of the evening it became clear that we were all using the same two words (forward deployed) to describe jobs that had almost nothing in common. In one part of the conversation an FDE was a sales engineer who joined ‘the second call,’ somewhere else it was a quota-carrying rep who could write Python, and a few seats down it was closer to a consultant with a laptop and a statement of work, brought in to deliver something the product couldn’t.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Ganesh goes on to explain his view of what an FDE should do, and it’s all sensible stuff (albeit written with rather more LLM-voice than I would prefer). He says the FDEs job is to understand the business, to “collect nouns and verbs”, which mirrors what the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/DomainDrivenDesign.html">Domain-Driven Design</a> folks have been doing since before Eric wrote the blue book.</p>

<p>Despite all my eyeball rolling at this, the FDE meme is pushing for something valuable. Yes, it’s easy for me to remark that it’s nothing more than the <a href="https://agilemanifesto.org/principles.html">Agile Manifesto’s principle</a> that “Business people and developers must work
together daily throughout the project”, or the desire to co-locate users and developers which my colleagues have been championing for all of this century. I’ve argued for decades that the biggest issue in software development is the communication between developers and the folks that benefit from software, and thus we need to focus on bridging the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/videos.html#y2007-crevasse">yawning crevasse of doom</a>. But despite all this, we haven’t had much success, so I think it’s important that a new generation of pundits try again, with some different framing, names, and slogans.</p>

<p>Ganesh’s perspective is not a custom software developer’s point of view, but rather a product - or more strictly - platform team’s. The FDE is a developer who “sits with” their users, applies customizations - but importantly - feeds these changes back to the core platform to decide whether the platform should be enhanced for everyone else.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Keeping the customer happy is a real job and a good one. It belongs to solutions architects, who are rightly measured on it. The forward deployed engineer is there to turn what the field teaches into the thing every future customer gets. An FDE engagement that ends with one delighted account and nothing changed upstream has failed at the only thing the role exists for. You got the context and you spent it locally.</p>
</blockquote>

---

### [Healthy Feedback](https://martinfowler.com/articles/healthy-peer-feedback.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/healthy-peer-feedback.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/healthy-peer-feedback/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

      not obvious how to make feedback effective. <b class="author">Anuja Karnik</b>
      and <b class="author">Sumeet Gayathri Moghe</b> pass on a bevy of tips for healthy
      feedback, based on the principle that both praise and criticism should be
      seen as positive.</p>
